Quasisymmetries of finitely ramified Julia sets
Authors: James Belk, Bradley Forrest
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
We develop a theory of quasisymmetries for finitely ramified fractals, with applications to finitely ramified Julia sets. We prove that certain finitely ramified fractals admit a naturally defined class of “undistorted metrics” that are all quasisymmetrically equivalent. As a result, piecewise-defined homeomorphisms of such a fractal that locally preserve the cell structure are quasisymmetries. This immediately gives a solution to the quasisymmetric uniformization problem for topologically rigid fractals such as the Sierpiński triangle. We show that our theory applies to many finitely ramified Julia sets, and we prove that any connected Julia set for a hyperbolic unicritical polynomial has infinitely many quasisymmetries, generalizing a result of Lyubich and Merenkov. We also prove that the quasisymmetry group of the Julia set for the rational function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-z^{-2}$$\end{document} is infinite, and we show that the quasisymmetry groups for the Julia sets of a broad class of polynomials contain Thompson’s group F.

Introduction

Classical quasiconformal geometry is concerned with quasiconformal maps between open subsets of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 2$$\end{document}), but the definition of a quasiconformal map does not generalize well to arbitrary metric spaces. In 1980, Pekka Tukia and Jussi Väisälä introduced a class of homeomorphisms between arbitrary metric spaces that they called quasisymmetries [64]. These maps are closely related to quasiconformal homeomorphisms on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document}, but the definition makes sense for arbitrary metric spaces, allowing for the study quasiconformal geometry in a very general setting.

Over the last two decades, the increasing importance of quasiconformal geometry in topology and analysis has ignited significant interest in quasisymmetries of fractals [10]. Such maps arise naturally in the study of quasi-isometries of Gromov hyperbolic spaces [19], and give a possible approach to Cannon’s conjecture for Gromov hyperbolic groups [13]. Quasisymmetries also arise in the study of postcritically finite rational maps in complex dynamics [43].

A metric space is quasisymmetrically rigid if its group of quasisymmetries is finite. In 2013, Mario Bonk and Sergei Merenkov proved the surprising result that the square Sierpiński carpet is quasisymmetrically rigid [15]. In 2016 Bonk, Merenkov, and Mikhail Lyubich proved the same result for certain Sierpiński carpet Julia sets [14], a result which has since been generalized [54]. In 2019, Lyubich and Merenkov gave the first example of a multiply connected Julia set with infinitely many quasisymmetries, namely the “basilica” Julia set for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^2-1$$\end{document} [39]. Recently Russell Lodge, Lyubich, Merenkov, and Sabyasachi Mukherjee gave examples of Apollonian gasket Julia sets with infinitely many quasisymmetries [37] and Timothy Alland showed that the Feigenbaum quadratic Julia set has infinitely many quasisymmetries [1].

The most important geometric difference between a Sierpiński carpet and Julia sets such as the basilica or Apollonian gasket is that the basilica and Apollonian gasket are finitely ramified fractals. Roughly speaking, this means that these Julia sets have a tree of subsets called “cells”, where each cell intersects neighboring cells at finitely many points. A precise definition of finitely ramified fractal was introduced by Alexander Teplyaev in 2008 [63] as a common generalization of Kigami’s postcritically finite self-similar sets [35] and Strichartz’s fractafolds [60]. Many other Julia sets are finitely ramified, as are certain well-known fractals such as the Sierpiński triangle and the Viscek fractal.

In this paper we develop a general theory of quasisymmetries for finitely ramified fractals. Though Teplyaev’s definition is entirely topological, we show that certain finitely ramified fractals have a distinguished quasisymmetric equivalence class of metrics that we call undistorted metrics, and we give a complete characterization of quasisymmetries with respect to such metrics. Among other consequences, this yields a solution to the quasisymmetric uniformization problem for “topologically rigid” finitely ramified fractals such as the Sierpiński triangle. We also define a group of homeomorphisms of a finitely ramified fractal that we call piecewise cellular, and we show that such homeomorphisms are always quasisymmetries when the metric is undistorted.

Next we apply this theory to Julia sets for hyperbolic rational maps. First, we give sufficient conditions for the Julia set to be finitely ramified, and we prove that under these conditions the restriction of the usual metric on the Riemann sphere is undistorted. This means that piecewise cellular homeomorphisms of such Julia sets are quasisymmetries, and we illustrate this result by proving that the Julia set for the rational map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z^{-2}-1$$\end{document} has infinitely many quasisymmetries. Finally we specialize to hyperbolic polynomials. We prove that any connected Julia set for a hyperbolic polynomial is finitely ramified, and we investigate which polynomial Julia sets have infinitely many quasisymmetries. We have two results along these lines, the first of which is the following.

Theorem 1

If f is a unicritical polynomial whose critical point is periodic, then the Julia set for f has infinitely many quasisymmetries.

Here a polynomial is unicritical if it has only one critical point, e.g. any quadratic polynomial. We prove Theorem 1 by showing that the quasisymmetry group for a unicritical polynomial with periodic critical point contains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_m*\mathbb {Z}_n$$\end{document} for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m,n\ge 2$$\end{document}. Our second result is that the quasisymmetry groups of the Julia sets for a certain family of hyperbolic polynomials contain Thompson’s group F. This family includes the “generic” case where all critical points are simple and periodic with disjoint cycles. In particular, we prove the following.

Theorem 2

Let f be a hyperbolic quadratic polynomial with connected Julia set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}. Then the quasisymmetry group of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} contains Thompson’s group F.

The rest of the introduction is organized as follows. We begin in Sect. 1.1 with an outline of the main theory: including our definition of finitely ramified fractals (which is slightly different from Teplyaev’s); a discussion of finitely ramified Julia sets; the definitions of undistorted metrics and piecewise cellular homeomorphisms; and statements of our main theorems about quasisymmetries of finitely ramified fractals. In Sect. 1.2, we apply this theory to the quasisymmetric uniformization of topologically rigid fractals such as the Sierpiński triangle. Finally, in Sect. 1.3 we give a general procedure for constructing quasisymmetries of Julia sets of hyperbolic rational maps, we state our main theorems regarding polynomial Julia sets with infinitely many quasisymmetries, and we discuss the consequences for cubic polynomials.

Outline of the theory

Here we outline the theory of quasisymmetries of finitely ramified fractals, including statements of our general results about quasisymmetries of such fractals.

Finitely ramified fractals

The idea of a finitely ramified fractal first arose in a 1981 article by Gefen, Aharony, Mandelbrot, and Kirkpatrick on the physics of percolation [27]. They used the term “finitely ramified” to refer to fractals that are made up of many small pieces that are connected to the rest of the fractal at only finitely many points. In 2008, Teplyaev formalized this notion by introducing finitely ramified cell structures [63, Definition 2.1]. We will use the following definition, which is slightly different from Teplyaev’s.

Definition 3

Given a compact, connected, metrizable space X, a finitely ramified cell structure on X consists of a locally finite, rooted tree of subsets of X called cells, satisfying the following conditions: Every cell is compact, connected, and has nonempty topological interior.The root of the tree is the whole space X, and each cell is the union of its children.The intersection of any two cells at the same level is a finite set.For every descending path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=E_0\supseteq E_1\supseteq E_2\supseteq \cdots $$\end{document} in the tree, the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcap _{n\ge 1} E_n$$\end{document} is a single point.We will refer to the cells at level n of the tree as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{n}$$\end{document}-cells.

Every cell is compact, connected, and has nonempty topological interior.

Every cell is compact, connected, and has nonempty topological interior.

The root of the tree is the whole space X, and each cell is the union of its children.

The root of the tree is the whole space X, and each cell is the union of its children.

The intersection of any two cells at the same level is a finite set.

The intersection of any two cells at the same level is a finite set.

For every descending path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=E_0\supseteq E_1\supseteq E_2\supseteq \cdots $$\end{document} in the tree, the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcap _{n\ge 1} E_n$$\end{document} is a single point.

For every descending path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X=E_0\supseteq E_1\supseteq E_2\supseteq \cdots $$\end{document} in the tree, the intersection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigcap _{n\ge 1} E_n$$\end{document} is a single point.

For example, Fig. 1 shows a finitely ramified cell structure on the Sierpiński triangle. A metrizable space X together with a finitely ramified cell structure on X is called a finitely ramified fractal.

The main difference1 between the above definition and Teplyaev’s is that we do not require the cells at level \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n+1$$\end{document} to be distinct from those at level n, since such a restriction would be inconvenient for defining cell structures on Julia sets. Despite this convention, it will be helpful for notation to regard all of the cells in the tree as distinct objects. Thus our “cells” will really be ordered pairs (E, n), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\subseteq X$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document} determines the level of the cell.

The notion of a finitely ramified fractal is similar to, but more general than, the notion of a postcritically finite (p.c.f.) fractal introduced by Kigami [35]. See [63, Example 8.9] for an example of a fractal which is finitely ramified but postcritically infinite.

Fig. 1The Sierpiński triangle has a natural finitely ramified cell structure with one 0-cell, three 1-cells, nine 2-cells, and so forth

The Sierpiński triangle has a natural finitely ramified cell structure with one 0-cell, three 1-cells, nine 2-cells, and so forth

Finitely ramified Julia sets

Recall that every rational map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\widehat{\mathbb {C}}\rightarrow \widehat{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document} has an associated Julia set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}, which can be defined as the closure of the set of repelling periodic points of f. This set is completely invariant under f, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}(J_f)=f(J_f)=J_f$$\end{document}. We are interested in conditions under which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} has the structure of a finitely ramified fractal.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} is connected, define a branch cut for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} to be any closed subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\subseteq J_f$$\end{document} for which f is injective on each component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus f^{-1}(S)$$\end{document}. (Note then that each component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus S$$\end{document} is the domain for a single-valued branch of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}$$\end{document}.) Such a branch cut is finite if S is a finite set, and invariant if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(S)\subseteq S$$\end{document}. The following basic theorem is proven in Sect. 3.1.

Theorem 4

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\widehat{\mathbb {C}}\rightarrow \widehat{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document} be a hyperbolic rational map with connected Julia set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} has a finite invariant branch cut S, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} admits a finitely ramified cell structure whose n-cells (for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 1$$\end{document}) are the closures of the connected components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus f^{-n}(S)$$\end{document}.

We will refer to the finitely ramified cell structure described by this theorem as the induced finitely ramified cell structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}.

In the statement of Theorem 4, a rational map f is hyperbolic if it is expanding on a neighborhood of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} with respect to some conformal metric (see [46, §19]). Equivalently, f is hyperbolic if the forward orbit of every critical point converges to an attracting cycle [46, Theorem 19.1]. Julia sets for hyperbolic rational maps behave much better than arbitrary Julia sets, e.g. if f is hyperbolic and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} is connected, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} is also locally connected [46, Theorem 19.2] and the restriction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:J_f\rightarrow J_f$$\end{document} is a covering map. Hyperbolic maps are conjectured to form a dense open set in the parameter space of all rational maps [41, Conjecture HD], and in particular a quadratic polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z)=z^2+c$$\end{document} is conjectured to be hyperbolic if and only if c does not lie on the boundary of the Mandelbrot set [41, Conjecture HD2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$'$$\end{document}].Fig. 2A finitely ramified cell structure for the basilica Julia set with four 1-cells, eight 2-cells, and sixteen 3-cells

A finitely ramified cell structure for the basilica Julia set with four 1-cells, eight 2-cells, and sixteen 3-cells

Example 5

(The basilica) The Julia set for the polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z)=z^2-1$$\end{document} is known as the basilica. This polynomial has two fixed points, one of which is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\bigl (1-\sqrt{5}\hspace{0.08333em}\bigr )\bigr /2 \approx -0.618$$\end{document}. The one-point set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{p\}$$\end{document} is a finite invariant branch cut for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}. By Theorem 4, there is a corresponding finitely ramified cell structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} whose n-cells are the closures of the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f{\setminus } f^{-n}(p)$$\end{document}. The 1-cells, 2-cells, and 3-cells for this cell structure are shown in Fig. 2.

It is not hard to show that a connected Julia set for a hyperbolic polynomial always admits a finite invariant branch cut (see Proposition 3.7), and hence connected Julia sets for hyperbolic polynomials are always finitely ramified. However, Theorem 4 also defines finitely ramified cell structures for the Julia sets of certain non-polynomial rational maps.Fig. 3A finitely ramified cell structure for the bubble bath Julia set with six 1-cells and twelve 2-cells

A finitely ramified cell structure for the bubble bath Julia set with six 1-cells and twelve 2-cells

Example 6

(The bubble bath) The rational map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z)= 1-z^{-2}$$\end{document} is hyperbolic and has a connected Julia set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}, which we refer to as the bubble bath (see [67]). This map has a real fixed point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\approx -0.7549$$\end{document} and two complex conjugate fixed points\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q\approx 0.8774+0.7449i\qquad \text {and}\qquad {\overline{q}}\approx 0.8774-0.7449i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}and we prove in Sect. 3.4 that the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{q,{\overline{q}}\}$$\end{document} is a branch cut for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}. By Theorem 4, there is a corresponding finitely ramified cell structure on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} whose n-cells are the closures of the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus f^{-n}(p)$$\end{document}. The 1-cells and 2-cells for this cell structure are shown in Fig. 3.

Remark 7

If S is a finite invariant branch cut for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}, then the closures of the components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus S$$\end{document} (i.e. the 1-cells) form a Markov partition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} in the sense of Ruelle [55]. From this point of view, Theorem 4 is a version of the familiar fact that a Markov partition for an expanding map has a well-defined coding by a subshift of finite type. In the case of quadratic polynomials, a similar strategy is used to define the well-known Yoccoz puzzle [31].

Quasisymmetries

A homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:X\rightarrow Y$$\end{document} between metric spaces is called a quasisymmetry if there exists a homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta:[0,\infty )\rightarrow [0,\infty )$$\end{document} such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\frac{d_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}\bigl (f(a),f(b)\bigr )}{d_{\scriptscriptstyle Y}\bigl (f(a),f(c)\bigr )} \le \eta \biggl (\frac{d_{\scriptscriptstyle X}(a,b)}{d_{\scriptscriptstyle X}(a,c)}\biggr )\end{aligned}$$\end{document}for every triple a, b, c of distinct points in X. A homeomorphism f which is quasisymmetric with respect to a given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document} is called an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{\eta }$$\end{document}-quasisymmetry.

The quasisymmetry condition given above first appeared in the work of Beurling and Ahlfors [8], who proved that the quasisymmetries of a circle are precisely the restrictions of the quasiconformal homeomorphisms of a closed disk to its boundary. The term “quasisymmetry” was later coined by Kelingos [34], and the modern definition of a quasisymmetric homeomorphism between metric spaces was given by Tukia and Väisälä in 1980 [64]. Väisälä established a strong general relationship between quasiconformal homeomorphisms and quasisymmetries with his “egg yolk principle” [66], which implies that every quasiconformal homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:U\rightarrow V$$\end{document} between domains in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} restricts to a quasisymmetry on each compact subset of U. See [30] for a general introduction to quasisymmetries.

Compositions and inverses of quasisymmetries are again quasisymmetries, and therefore the self-quasisymmetries of any metric space X form a group QS(X). Two metric spaces X and Y are said to be quasisymmetrically equivalent if there exists a quasisymmetry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\rightarrow Y$$\end{document}, in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QS(X)\cong QS(Y)$$\end{document}. Similarly, two metrics d and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d'$$\end{document} on a metrizable space X are quasisymmetrically equivalent if the identity map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X,d)\rightarrow (X,d')$$\end{document} is a quasisymmetry, in which case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$QS(X,d) = QS(X,d')$$\end{document}.

Undistorted metrics

Our main general result about finitely ramified fractals is to prove that certain finitely ramified fractals admit a natural family of quasisymmetrically equivalent metrics.

Definition 8

If X is a finitely ramified fractal, we say that a metric d on X is undistorted if it satisfies the following conditions: Exponential decay: There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< r \le R < 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C\ge 1$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\frac{r^{n-m}}{C} \le \frac{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E')}{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E)} \le C R^{n-m}\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every m-cell E and n-cell \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E'$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge m$$\end{document} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\cap E'\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}.Cell separation: There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}d(E_1,E_2) \ge \delta {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E_1)\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,E_2$$\end{document} of disjoint n-cells.

Exponential decay: There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< r \le R < 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C\ge 1$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\frac{r^{n-m}}{C} \le \frac{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E')}{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E)} \le C R^{n-m}\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every m-cell E and n-cell \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E'$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge m$$\end{document} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\cap E'\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}.

Exponential decay: There exist constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< r \le R < 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C\ge 1$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\frac{r^{n-m}}{C} \le \frac{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E')}{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E)} \le C R^{n-m}\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every m-cell E and n-cell \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E'$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge m$$\end{document} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E\cap E'\ne \emptyset $$\end{document}.

Cell separation: There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}d(E_1,E_2) \ge \delta {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E_1)\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,E_2$$\end{document} of disjoint n-cells.

Cell separation: There exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta >0$$\end{document} so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}d(E_1,E_2) \ge \delta {{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E_1)\end{aligned}$$\end{document} for every pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,E_2$$\end{document} of disjoint n-cells.

If we wish to specify the constants r, R, C, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}, then we will say that the metric d is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{(r,R,C,\delta )}$$\end{document}-undistorted. For example, it is easy to check that the restriction of the Euclidean metric to the Sierpiński triangle is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bigl (1/2, 1/2, 1, \sqrt{3}/2\bigr )$$\end{document}-undistorted with respect to the cell structure shown in Fig. 1.

Note that the cell separation condition could be written\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}d(E_1,E_2)\ge \delta \max \{{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E_1),{{\,\textrm{diam}\,}}(E_2)\}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}This condition is very similar to the “uniform relative separation” condition introduced by Bonk for the uniformization of plane Sierpiński carpets [11], though we compare only cells of the same level and we use a max instead of a min.

Theorem 9

Let X be a finitely ramified fractal. Then: Any two undistorted metrics on X are quasisymmetrically equivalent.If a metric d on X is undistorted, then so is any metric which is quasisymmetrically equivalent to d.

Any two undistorted metrics on X are quasisymmetrically equivalent.

Any two undistorted metrics on X are quasisymmetrically equivalent.

If a metric d on X is undistorted, then so is any metric which is quasisymmetrically equivalent to d.

If a metric d on X is undistorted, then so is any metric which is quasisymmetrically equivalent to d.

We prove this theorem in Sect. 2.1. Not every finitely ramified fractal X admits an undistorted metric (see Theorem 11 below), but if such a metric exists then Theorem 9 tells us that it is unique up to quasisymmetric equivalence. In particular, the quasisymmetry group QS(X) with respect to an undistorted metric does not depend on the choice of undistorted metric, but instead depends only on the topology of X together with the cell structure. It would be interesting to find an explicit topological characterization of quasisymmetries in this case.

The following example illustrates undistorted metrics and Theorem 9.Fig. 4The first three steps in the construction of the self-affine fractal V(a, b, c)Fig. 5Three fractals in the V(a, b, c) family, the first of which is the famous Vicsek fractal. The Euclidean metric is undistorted for the first two, but distorted for the third

The first three steps in the construction of the self-affine fractal V(a, b, c)

Three fractals in the V(a, b, c) family, the first of which is the famous Vicsek fractal. The Euclidean metric is undistorted for the first two, but distorted for the third

Example 10

(The Vicsek Family) Given real numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a,b,c\in (0,1)$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a+b+c=1$$\end{document}, let V(a, b, c) be the self-affine fractal constructed using the procedure shown in Fig. 4, where we iteratively replace each rectangle with five subrectangles whose side lengths have the given ratios. Figure 5 shows three examples of the resulting fractal V(a, b, c) for different values of the parameters, with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a,b,c)=(1/3,1/3,1/3)$$\end{document} case being the famous Vicsek fractal.

Each of the fractals V(a, b, c) has a natural finitely ramified cell structure with exactly \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$5^n$$\end{document} different n-cells for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 0$$\end{document}. In the case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a=c$$\end{document}, it is easy to check that the Euclidean metric restricts to an undistorted metric on V(a, b, c), so all such fractals are quasisymmetrically equivalent, even though the obvious homeomorphisms between these fractals are not bilipschitz. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\ne c$$\end{document}, the restriction of the Euclidean metric to V(a, b, c) is distorted (i.e. not undistorted), since for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 0$$\end{document} there exist a pair of intersecting n-cells with diameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{2}\,ac^{n-1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sqrt{2}\,ba^{n-1}$$\end{document}, respectively.

The following theorem (proven in Sect. 2.2) characterizes the finitely ramified fractals that admit undistorted metrics.

Theorem 11

Let X be a finitely ramified fractal. Then X admits an undistorted metric if and only if there exists a constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document} that satisfies the following conditions: Every n-cell in X contains at least two disjoint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cells.No two disjoint n-cells in X intersect a common \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cell.

Every n-cell in X contains at least two disjoint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cells.

Every n-cell in X contains at least two disjoint \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cells.

No two disjoint n-cells in X intersect a common \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cell.

No two disjoint n-cells in X intersect a common \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cell.

The two conditions in Theorem 11 are combinatorial versions of exponential decay and cell separation respectively. Indeed, both exponential decay and cell separation are uniform conditions and Theorem 11 also enforces this uniformity. While we will focus our attention on finitely ramified fractals with undistorted metrics, Example 2.4 presents a finitely ramified fractal that does not admit an undistorted metric.

It is easy to check that the limit spaces for expanding replacement systems defined in [6] satisfy the two conditions above, and therefore admit undistorted metrics.

Piecewise cellular homeomorphisms

As a consequence of Theorem 9, we get the following useful test for whether a given homeomorphism is a quasisymmetry.

Corollary 12

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X,d_X)$$\end{document} be a finitely ramified fractal with undistorted metric, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Y,d_Y)$$\end{document} be a metric space, and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:X\rightarrow Y$$\end{document} be a homeomorphism. Then f is a quasisymmetry if and only if the pullback metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^*(d_Y)$$\end{document} on X is undistorted.

That is, f is a quasisymmetry if and only if the images in Y of the cells of X satisfy the exponential decay condition and the cell separation condition.

We can use this corollary to define a large class of quasisymmetries between finitely ramified fractals. First, we say that a homeomorphism between an m-cell and an n-cell is cellular if it maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(m+k)$$\end{document}-cells to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+k)$$\end{document}-cells for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\ge 0$$\end{document}. A homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:X\rightarrow Y$$\end{document} between finitely ramified fractals is piecewise cellular if we can subdivide X and Y into cells \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,\ldots,E_k$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1',\ldots,E_k'$$\end{document}, respectively, such that f maps each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_i$$\end{document} cellularly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{i}'$$\end{document}.

Example 13

(Thompson’s group F) There is a finitely ramified cell structure on [0, 1] whose n-cells are the intervals\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \biggl [\dfrac{k-1}{2^n},\dfrac{k}{2^n}\biggr ] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1\le k\le 2^n$$\end{document}. Such cells are known as standard dyadic intervals, and a homeomorphism between two standard dyadic intervals is cellular if and only if it is linear. A homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:[0,1]\rightarrow [0,1]$$\end{document} is piecewise cellular if and only if there are subdivisions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1,\ldots,E_m$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_1',\ldots,E_m'$$\end{document} of [0, 1] into standard dyadic intervals such that f maps each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_i$$\end{document} linearly to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_i'$$\end{document}. Such an f must be either orientation-preserving or orientation-reversing, and the group of orientation-preserving piecewise cellular homeomorphisms of [0, 1] is Thompson’s group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{F}$$\end{document} (see [4, 22]).

Fig. 6A piecewise-cellular homeomorphism of the basilica Julia set (taken from [6]). Each cell in the domain maps to a corresponding cell in the range by a cellular homeomorphism

A piecewise-cellular homeomorphism of the basilica Julia set (taken from [6]). Each cell in the domain maps to a corresponding cell in the range by a cellular homeomorphism

Example 14

(The basilica Thompson group) Figure 6 shows a piecewise-cellular homeomorphism of the basilica Julia set. The domain and range have each been subdivided into six cells, and each domain cell maps to a corresponding range cell by a cellular homeomorphism. This is an element of the basilica Thompson group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{T_B}$$\end{document} defined by the authors in [5]. Lyubich and Merenkov subsequently proved that all elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_B$$\end{document} act on the basilica by quasisymmetries [39]. In [6], the authors generalized the basilica Thompson group to the class of rearrangement groups, which act on the corresponding limit space by piecewise-cellular homeomorphisms.

Note that there are many piecewise-cellular homeomorphisms of the basilica beyond the elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_B$$\end{document}. For example, we can “flip” any cell that has a single boundary point, or indeed any disjoint collection of such cells, and this will be a cellular homeomorphism of the basilica. It follows that the basilica has uncountably many cellular homeomorphisms.

We prove the following theorem in Sect. 2.4.

Theorem 15

Let X and Y be finitely ramified fractals with undistorted metrics. Then any piecewise cellular homeomorphism from X to Y is a quasisymmetry.

For a single finitely ramified fractal X with an undistorted metric, the piecewise cellular homeomorphisms \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\rightarrow X$$\end{document} form a group, and according to this theorem all such homeomorphisms are quasisymmetries of X. For example, it follows from this theorem that all of the rearrangement groups described in [6] act by quasisymmetries on the corresponding limit spaces.

Application to quasisymmetric uniformization

The quasisymmetric uniformization problem for a “model” metric space Y (or family of such spaces) asks how to tell whether a given metric space X which is homeomorphic to Y is quasisymmetrically equivalent to Y [10, Section 3]. For example, Tukia and Väisälä solved this problem in the case where Y is a closed interval or circle under the standard metric [64]. The case where Y is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^2$$\end{document} has been investigated by Semmes [56], by Bonk and Kleiner [12], and by Wildrick [68], but this remains an open area of research. Indeed, Cannon’s conjecture [23] is equivalent to the statement that any Gromov boundary of a hyperbolic group which is homeomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^2$$\end{document} must be quasisymmetrically equivalent to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^2$$\end{document} [10, Conjecture 5.2]. Quasisymmetric uniformization has been studied for other surfaces [28, 42], but appears to be very difficult for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^n$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^n$$\end{document} when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n\ge 3$$\end{document} [57].

We can also consider quasisymmetric uniformization for fractals. For example, David and Semmes gave necessary and sufficient conditions for a Cantor space to be quasisymmetrically equivalent to the middle-thirds Cantor set [25], and Bonk gave sufficient conditions for a Sierpiński carpet in the plane to be quasisymmetrically equivalent to a round carpet, i.e. one whose peripheral circles are all round circles [11]. Quasisymmetric uniformization for Sierpiński carpets is particularly interesting due to the Kapovich–Kleiner conjecture, an analog of Cannon’s conjecture for hyperbolic groups with Sierpiński carpet boundaries (see [32, Conjecture 6] and [10, Conjecture 7.4]). More recently, Bonk and Meyer gave necessary and sufficient conditions for a metric dendrite to be quasisymmetrically equivalent to a dendrite with a geodesic metric [17], and gave specific conditions for a metric dendrite to be quasisymmetrically equivalent to the continuum self-similar tree [18]. In addition, Qui and Yang have proven that Julia sets of hyperbolic rational maps that are Cantor circles are quasisymmetrically equivalent to a standard Cantor circle [53].

Our Theorem 9 gives a solution to the quasisymmetric uniformization problem for certain finitely ramified fractals. Specifically, we say that a finitely ramified fractal Y is topologically rigid if every homeomorphism from Y to itself preserves the cell structure. Bandt and Retta have shown that many well-known fractals are topologically rigid [3, Theorem 4.2], including the Sierpiński triangle, the n-dimensional Sierpiński gasket, the pentagasket and hexagasket (see [59]), and so forth. For example, the Sierpiński triangle is topologically rigid because the three points at which the 1-cells intersect are the only three points whose removal disconnects the fractal into three pieces, so any homeomorphism must permute these points and hence the 1-cells, and by induction the n-cells. As observed by Jasper Weinburd [67, Chapter 4], topological rigidity is also common for Julia sets of rational maps, with one such example shown in Fig. 7. Note that the Sierpiński triangle also arises as the Julia set of a rational map [65].Fig. 7The Julia set for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z) = (e^{2\pi i/3} z^2-1)/(z^2-1)$$\end{document} has a finitely ramified cell structure with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^n$$\end{document} different n-cells for each n. Every homeomorphism of the Julia set preserves this cell structure, and the homeomorphism group has order 8

The Julia set for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z) = (e^{2\pi i/3} z^2-1)/(z^2-1)$$\end{document} has a finitely ramified cell structure with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2^n$$\end{document} different n-cells for each n. Every homeomorphism of the Julia set preserves this cell structure, and the homeomorphism group has order 8

Given a finitely ramified fractal Y and a space X that is homeomorphic to Y, there is a finitely ramified cell structure on X obtained by pulling back the cell structure on Y. Furthermore, if Y is topologically rigid, the cell structure on X is canonical in that it is independent of the homeomorphism. As a corollary to Theorem 9, we obtain the following solution to the quasisymmetric uniformization problem for topologically rigid finitely ramified fractals.

Corollary 16

Let Y be a finitely ramified fractal which is topologically rigid and whose metric is undistorted, and let X be a metric space which is homeomorphic to Y. Then X is quasisymmetrically equivalent to Y if and only if the metric on X is undistorted (with respect to the cell structure on X induced by the homeomorphism).

Corollary 16 gives a solution to the quasisymmetric uniformization problem for the Sierpiński triangle and the n-dimensional Sierpiński gasket. This solution is nontrivial in the sense that there are many metrics on these fractals that are not quasisymmetrically equivalent to the standard ones. For example, Fig. 8a shows the Rauzy gasket (see [2]), which is defined using an iterated function system on a triangle similar to that of the Sierpiński triangle, except that the three maps are projective instead of affine linear. Though it is homeomorphic to the Sierpiński triangle, the Rauzy gasket has the property that the maximum diameter of an n-cell is on the order of 1/n. This violates the exponential decay condition for an undistorted metric, so it follows that the Rauzy gasket is not quasisymmetrically equivalent to the Sierpiński triangle. Similarly, Fig. 8b shows a fractal homeomorphic to the Sierpiński triangle which fails the cell separation condition near the points where the circular sectors are tangent.Fig. 8a The Rauzy gasket b A Sierpiński triangle whose three main subtriangles have been expanded to circular sectors via radial projection. Neither of these fractals is quasisymmetrically equivalent to a standard Sierpiński triangle

a The Rauzy gasket b A Sierpiński triangle whose three main subtriangles have been expanded to circular sectors via radial projection. Neither of these fractals is quasisymmetrically equivalent to a standard Sierpiński triangle

Remark 17

Note that any self-homeomorphism of a topologically rigid finitely ramified fractal must permute the cells at each level. It follows that every self-homeomorphism is a quasisymmetry with respect to any undistorted metric, and the full group of homeomorphisms must be residually finite. Indeed, many topologically rigid fractals have finite homeomorphism groups. For example, the homeomorphism group of the Sierpiński triangle is dihedral of order 6 [38], and the homeomorphism group of the Julia set in Fig. 7 is dihedral of order 8 (with a faithful action on the four 2-cells). Such fractals are trivially quasisymmetrically rigid.

Applications to Julia sets

As we previously mentioned, the Julia set for any hyperbolic polynomial has a finite invariant branch cut (see Proposition 3.7), as do the Julia sets for some hyperbolic rational maps such as the bubble bath. By Theorem 4, such Julia sets inherit a natural finitely ramified cell structure. The following theorem, which we prove in Sect. 3.3, lets us apply our theory of undistorted metrics to obtain quasisymmetries of such Julia sets.

Theorem 18

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\widehat{\mathbb {C}}\rightarrow \widehat{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document} be a hyperbolic rational function, and suppose that the Julia set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} is connected and has a finite invariant branch cut. Then the restriction of the spherical metric on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} is undistorted with respect to the induced finitely ramified cell structure.

Piecewise canonical homeomorphisms

Theorem 18 gives a large class of Julia sets whose metrics are undistorted. By Theorem 15, any piecewise cellular homeomorphism of such a Julia set is a quasisymmetry. As we will now describe, such Julia sets have a very natural class of piecewise cellular homeomorphisms which are also piecewise conformal.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\widehat{\mathbb {C}}\rightarrow \widehat{\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document} is a hyperbolic rational map and A and B are connected subsets of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document}, we say that a homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g:A\rightarrow B$$\end{document} is canonical if there exist positive integers m and n so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^m\circ g$$\end{document} agrees with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^n$$\end{document} on A, i.e. g is a branch of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-m}\circ f^n$$\end{document} on A. Note that restrictions, compositions, and inverses of canonical homeomorphisms are canonical, and that canonical homeomorphisms map periodic and preperiodic points of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f$$\end{document} to periodic and preperiodic points.Fig. 9An order-two quasisymmetry of the basilica. This homeomorphism is piecewise canonical with a single breakpoint at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\bigl (1-\sqrt{5}\bigr )\bigr /2$$\end{document}. The blue component maps to the red component by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z)=z^2-1$$\end{document}, and the red component maps to the blue component by a branch of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}$$\end{document}

An order-two quasisymmetry of the basilica. This homeomorphism is piecewise canonical with a single breakpoint at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p=\bigl (1-\sqrt{5}\bigr )\bigr /2$$\end{document}. The blue component maps to the red component by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z)=z^2-1$$\end{document}, and the red component maps to the blue component by a branch of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f^{-1}$$\end{document}

We say that a homeomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:J_f\rightarrow J_f$$\end{document} is piecewise canonical if there exists a finite set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B\subseteq J_f$$\end{document} of breakpoints such that h restricts to a canonical homeomorphism on each component of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J_f\setminus B$$\end{document}. For example, Fig. 9 shows a piecewise canonical homeomorphism of the basilica. (See [5] for many more such examples.) The following theorem, which we prove in Sect. 3.3, shows that piecewise canonical homeomorphisms are often quasisymmetries.

Theorem 19
